It is composed of the three star systems: Gamma Cancri, Delta Cancri, and Theta Cancri. In the Western zodiac it is equivalent to the very end of Cancer and nearly half of Leo—in other words, the end of July and the first part of August.\n\n(Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-33236.html)\n\nSynonyms: {rgyal}, {rgyal skar ma}37<term> Conqueror, jina, victor, Victorious One (Skt: jina): \n1) The most common epithet of the buddhas, and also common among the Jains, hence their name. 29{(tA la'i bla ma sku phreng lnga pa )'di nyid ni rgyal ba sku phreng rim byon gyi nang nas chos srid gnyis ka'i thog mdzad rjes dang mtshan snyan shin tu che ba'i gras shig yin/khong ni lho kha 43jayaghoṣaṇā — {rgyal ba bsgrags pa byas} vidadhe jayaghoṣaṇām a.ka.106.19.
2Dalai lama vii, 1708-185729{(tA la'i bla ma sku phreng bdun pa )khong ni rgyal ba sku phreng rim byon gyi nang nas mtshan snyan ha cang che ba'i gras shig yin/'khrungs yul ni mdo khams li thang byams pa gling gi lha zhol du rab byung bcu gnyis pa'i sa byi (1708)lor yab bsod nams dar rgyas dang yum bsod nams chos mtsho gnyis kyi sras su 'khrungs/dgung lo lnga bcu pa me glang lor sku gshegs/thi lang chos skyong nas rgyal ba'i yang srid yin pa'i lung bstan bzhin p+saku zhang pas dge bkra shis nas skal bzang rgya mtsho zhes mtshan gsol/gnas tshul de rim bzhin dbus gtsang tu khyab rjes/rgyal po lha bzang gis phyogs der lkog nyan byed mi mda' dpon nor bu dngos grub can mi gnyis btang/mda' dpon nor bu dgon grub kyi 'di tshangs dbyangs rgya mtsho'i sku skye yin zhes brjod na phan thogs med ces yongs grags su brjod pa dang gsung ba'i thog nas sku skye 'di sa khul gzhan zhig tu gdan zhu byas na lags tshul bslab bya yang byas par brten de nub rang yab kyis 'brog pa'i sa char gdan drangs shing/de nas shing rta (1714)lo'i zla 3tshes 14nyis sde dger gdan zhu byas/de'i gnas tshul mtsho sngon phyogs kyi sbyin bdag sog po ching wang ba thur tha'i ji sogs kyis thos te lha bzang khang gi tshangs dbyangs rgya mtshor mi 'tshams pa zhus pa dang/da dung yang srid la'ang gnod 'tshe btang dogs gdan zhu'i mi snang btang ste lo de rang du sde dge nas mtsho sngon dga' ldan e ti ni ju nang gi sdod sgar la phebs pa dang dbus phyogs nas gdan sa gsum dang/stag rtse sde srid sogs gzhung 'bangs bsam shes can rnams nas gsang stabs kyis yang srid rin po cher gzhung thog nas tA la'i bla mar ngos 'dzin zhus pa dang/bod sog yongs nas yang srid rin po che 'dir ngos 'dzin khas len zhus/dgong lo brgyad la phebs skabs man ching gong ma khang shis ni kyang sngar yab dus su tA la'i bla ma lnga pa chen po dang 'brel ba dam zab yod pa'i srol rgyun ltar/sku tshab chen btang gis rgya bod hor sogs kyi chibs hrag zab rgyas dang bcas yang srid rin po che sku 'bum byams pa gling du gdan drangs/rgyal dbang bsod nams rgya mtsho'i bzhugs khrel mnga' gsol gyi mdzad sgo dang sbrags/gong ma'i bka' phab pa'i nang don du/tA la'i bla ma'i sprul sku 'di de phyogs la phebs pa nas bzung nged sengs shin tu bde/nged dang bstan 'dzin chos rgyal gyi gdung rgyud thams cad sbyin bdag yin/sprul pa'i sku 'di tA la'i bla ma gong ma'i ming srid 'khrul med yin pas/mchog dman thams cad kyis gus 'dud zhu dgos dang/thams cad mkhyen pa 'dzam gling la nyi ma shar ba lta bur sbar mos 'geb thub pa mi yong bas/gong rang gi thugs rje dang 'phrin las kyi 'od zer sa steng thams cad la khyab pas sangs rgyas kyi ba stan pa dar zhing rgyas pa 'byung/zhes khyab bsgrags byas/de nas bzung/cha gan no yin han dang/chu bzang no min han sogs yongs 'dzin du bsten nas nang pa sangs rgyas pa'i gzhung chen po rnams la tshig spyi tsam ma min par gsan bsam gtso bor mdzad/mep+saparel lo'i hor zla 10nang sku 'bum dngul gdung chen mo'i drung du yongs 'dzin chu bzang rin po che'i drung du rab tu byung bar mdzad/mdo sngags gzhung lugs yongs la gnas bsam sgom sgrub thog /chos 'chad spel sogs kyi sgo nas gdul bya grangs las 'das pa bsam pa chos phyogs su 'god par mdzad/sbyin bdag gi gtso bo bstan 'dzin ching wang dang dga' ldan ju nang gis gtsos sog yul dang mtsho sngon phyogs yod pa'i dpon khag yod do cog nas sku 'bum du yod pa'i sprul sku rim po che dbus phyogs su gdan 'dren zhu rgyu'i gros mthun byung ba dang/ching gong mas kyang rje bla ma ma chog nyid dbus gtsang phyogs su gdan drangs te pho brang chen po po ta la'i gser khrir mnga' gsol dgos tshul gyi bka' rgya dang mi sna 'byor ba'i mdun tA la'i bla ma sku phreng lnga pa chen po'i dus kyi srol ltar 'ja' sa tham ka sogs kyang phul/dgung lo bcu gsum dus sku 'bum byams pa gling nas bod phyogs su chibs kha bsgyur ba'i tshe/gong ma'i sras bcu bzhi pa cang jun phebs skyel du mngags pa dang de bzhin sog po dang mtsho sngon khul gyi sbyin bdag nang/gong ma'i sku tshab tu thu'u bkwan ho thog thu/bka' 'gyur tA bla ma/khal kha don grub wang sogs shin tu gtos che ba'i chibs zhabs dang/rgya sog gi sku srung dmag mi khyon che dang bcas/lcags byi lo'i zla dgu pa'i tshes bco lnga nyin pho brang chen po po ta lar phebs/zla 11tshes 5nyin po ti lar paN chen blo bzang ye shes nas mkhan slob sbrags ma mdzad de dag tshul gyi sdom pa bzhes/mtshan la blo bzang skal bzang rgya mtsho zhes gsol/skabs de'i tshe bod gzhung nas mdzad sgo khag bcas la gong ma'i dpon rigs dang/bod kyi dpon rigs rnam mdun gral/sog po'i dpon rigs rnams rgyab gral du bzhag pas gral gnas blor ma bab pa'i thog /sde srid stag rtse'i srog bskyabs pa yang don smin ma byung ba/sngar mi dga' ba'i rnam 'gyur yang yang bstan pa dang/lhag par mtsho sngon phyogs su gzhis chags sog po dang a mdo ba rnams mnyam 'brel yis ching gong mar ngo rgol byed rgyu'i gra sgrig byas par brten/bod chu yos (1723)lor man ching gong ma yung kreng khri lo dang por lha sdod a sa am ban gyis gtsos rgya sog gi dpon dmag tshang ma phyir 'then byas/lo 'dir mtsho sngon phyogs gu shri han gyi sras rgyud dang/rgya sog chings byed par dga' ldan khri rin po che dpal ldan grags pa dang mgon gnyer blo bzang dkon mchog chen rdzongs mdzad de/dpon khag rnams nas rgyal bstan chab srid kyi lar rgyar bsam shes byas te 'phral phugs bde 'jags dgos pa'i bka' yig nang yang phan thogs ma byung/skabs der rgya'i dpon dpon nyar gung ya'o dang/yo tsung ci sogs kyis rgya dpag grangs las 'das pa khrid de mtsho sngon du dmag drangs thog gser khog dgon dang/dgon lus/sku bum sogs a mdo khul gyi dgon khag mang po gtor bcom dang mi sde khri khrag mang por gnod 'tshe/skabs de'i sku 'bum mkhan po chu bzang rin po che gson por mer ba sregs/se skya pho mo mang po bsad rmas btang bcas byung bar brten/rgyal dbang mchog nas man ching gong mar dmag mtshams 'phral du 'jog dgos skor thad kar phyag bris dang/thu'u bkwan rin po che ngag dbang chos kyi rgya mtsho brgyud thengs mang 'bod bskul gnang bcas kyi thugs rjes dmag mtshams bzhag ste/gong ma'i mdzod nas gong gsal gtor bshig byung ba'i dgon khag nyams gso byed rgyu'i bka' phab pa ma zad/bod gzhung nas kyang dgon sde tshang mar nyams gso'i mthun rkyen dang/bzo rigs mkhas pa mang po mngags ste rogs ram rgya cher byas yod/bod mi ser skya mchog dman tshang mas 2biography of dge 'dun rgya mtsho, Dalai lama II - (1476-1542)
2Mind Lineage of the Victorious Ones, The Buddha's Mind Transmission, the Jinas' mind lineage / transmission, the Buddha Thought Lineage.; the direct mind to mind transmission of the Jinas; mind transmission of the Buddha2Mind Lineage of the Victorious Ones. The first of the three lineages of the Nyingma School. Usually, it refers to the transmission from Buddha Samantabhadra to Garab Dorje of teachings beyond words and symbols8mind-transmission of the buddhas9mind lineage of the victorious 1s, the buddha's mind transmission, lineage of buddha-overall-intentionality, the jinas's mind lineage/ transmission, 'the buddha thought lineage'47{ston pa kun bzang rdo rje 'chang chen pos/ lhun grub 'og min gyi zhing khams chen por/ nyid las mi gzhan pa'i 'khor la/ chos dbyangs ye shes 'od gsal ba'i dgongs ba brjod du med pa nyid rang snang ba'i tshul gyis ston pa/}
491. {ston pas gsungs tshul ni/ rang snang rnam dag 'og min chos dbyings su/ /kun tu bzang pos ye shes rgya mtsho la/ / 'od gsal 'dus ma byas pa'i dgongs pa ni/ / bsam mi khyab dus rang bzhin snang bar mdzad/ / ston pa la chos sku gzugs sku'i rnam gzhag gnyis su yod pa las/} (Source: 《{shes bya kun khyab }》{deb stod cha sha }385.11{las btus/}).

54{khong ni rab byung gnyis pa'i chu mo lug lo ste spyi lo} 1103 {lor sku 'khrungs/} {rab byung gsum pa'i shing mo lug lo ste spyi lo} 1175 {lor sku gshegs/} {khong dgung lo drug cu rtsa gnyis pa rab byung gsum pa'i shing pho sprel lo ste spyi lo} 1164 {lor rgyal lha khang gi gdan sar phebs shing lo ngo bdun gyi ring du gdan sa bskyangs te slob ma mang du gso skyong mdzad/} {de nas 'gro don mthar zhog ste dgung lo bdun cu don gsum par dgongs pa chos dbyings su gshegs/} {khong ni rgyal lha khang gi gdan rabs brgyad pa yin no/} {rab byung gsum pa'i me pho sprel lo ste spyi lo} 1176 {lo nas rab byung gsum pa'i sa pho khyi lo ste spyi lo} 1178 {lo'i bar du rgyal lha khang gi gdan sa stongs ba 'gos lo gzhon nu dpal gyis gsungs so/ /}
2Gyalwa Longchen Rabjam :1308-1363
2Longchen Rabjam Drimey Özer (1308-1363), one of the most eminent masters of the Nyingma tradition
2Longchen Rabjam; Drimey Özer, 1308-1369. A major lineage master and writer of the Nyingma lineage. Longchen Rabjam was an incarnation of Princess Pema Sal, the daughter of King Trisong Deutsen, to whom Guru Rinpoche had entrusted his own lineage of Dzogchen known as Khandro Nyingtig. He is single-handedly regarded as the most important writer on Dzogchen teachings. His works include the Seven Great Treasuries, the Three Trilogies and his commentaries in the Nyingtig Yabshi

2Gyalwa Chog-Yang, one of Guru Padmasambhava twenty-five main disciples2Gyalwa Cho-yang. A close disciple of Guru Rinpoche who attained accomplishment through the practice of Hayagriva and later was incarnated as the Karmapas. Born in clan of Nganlam in the Phen Valley, he took ordination from Khenpo Bodhisattva in the first group of seven Tibetan monks. It is said that he kept his vows with utmost purity. Having received the transmission of Hayagriva from Padmasambhava, he practiced in solitude and reached the level of a vidyadhara9gyalwa choyang26{sngar chos rgyal khri srong lde'u btsan skabs bod kyi sad mi mi bdun gyi ya gyal zhig}29{(ngan lam rgyal ba mchog dbyangs kyang zhu )chos rgyal khri srong lde btsan gyi sku dus su byung ba'i sad mi bdun gyi ya gyal/'di nyid kyi 'gyur la/bstan 'gyur las/('phags pa 'jig rten dbang phyug yi ge drug pa'i sgrub thabs yid bzhin nor bu )zhes bya ba slob dpon 'phags pa klu sgrub kyis mdzad pa zhig }53{sngar chos rgyal khri srong lde'u btsan skabs bod kyi sad mi mi bdun gyi ya gyal zhig}55{sngar chos rgyal khri srong lde'u btsan skabs bod kyi sad mi mi bdun gyi ya gyal zhig}

9ruling circle/ clique
36<place> Heaven of the Four Great Kings37<place> Heaven of the Four Great Kings (Skt: caturmahārājika): One of the heavens of Buddhist cosmology, lowest among the six heavens of the desire realm (kāmadhātu, ' dod khams). Dwelling place of the Four Great Kings (caturmahārāja, rgyal chen bzhi), traditionally located on a terrace of Sumeru, just below the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. Each cardinal direction is ruled by one of the Four Great Kings and inhabited by a different class of nonhuman beings as their subjects: in the east, Dhṛtarāṣṭra rules the gandharvas; in the south, Virūḍhaka rules the kumbhāṇḍas; in the west, Virūpākṣa rules the nāgas; and in the north, Vaiśravaṇa rules the yakṣas. (Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-43980.html)\n\nSynonyms: {rgyal chen bzhi pa'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha rnams}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha'i ris}, {rgyal chen rigs bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi'i ris}38Synonyms: {rgyal chen bzhi pa'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha rnams}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris}, {rgyal chen rigs bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi'i ris}39<place> caturmahārājika

2Jetavana Grove23jetavanam36<term> garden of Prince Jeta, Jeta Grove, Jetavana, Jeta's Grove, Prince Jeta's Grove37<term> garden of Prince Jeta, Jeta Grove, Jetavana, Jeta's Grove, Prince Jeta's Grove (Skt: jetavana): A park in Śrāvastī, the capital of the ancient kingdom of Kośala in northern India. It was owned by Prince Jeta, and the wealthy merchant Anāthapiṇḍada, wishing to offer it to the Buddha, bought it from him by covering the entire property with gold coins. It was to become the place where the monks could be housed during the monsoon season, thus creating the first Buddhist monastery. It is therefore the setting for many of the Buddha's discourses. (Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-44205.html)\n\nSynonyms: {dze ta}, {dze ta'i tshal}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed}, {rgyal byed kyi tshal}, {rgyal byed tshal}38Synonyms: {dze ta'i tshal}, {dze ta}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed}, {rgyal byed kyi tshal}, {rgyal byed tshal}39<term> jetavana43nā. jetavanam, sthānam — śrāvastyāṃ viharati jetavane'nāthapiṇḍadasyārāme a.śa.13ka/11.45Sanskrit: Jetavana <Place Name>\n\n\nTibetan renderings of Jetavana: {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal/}, {rgyal byed tshal/}, {rgyal byed kyi tshal/}\n\n‘Jetri’s wood,’ name of a grove near Śrāvastī (where Buddha promulgated his doctrines).\n(normally) nt., rarely m. 36<place> Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park37<place> Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park (Skt: jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ): \n1) One of the first Buddhist monasteries, located in a park outside Śrāvastī, the capital of the ancient kingdom of Kośala in northern India. This park was originally owned by Prince Jeta, hence the name Jetavana, meaning Jeta's grove. The wealthy merchant Anāthapiṇḍada, wishing to offer it to the Buddha, sought to buy it from him, but the prince, not wishing to sell, said he would only do so if Anāthapiṇḍada covered the entire property with gold coins. Anāthapiṇḍada agreed, and managed to cover all of the park except the entrance, hence the name Anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, meaning Anāthapiṇḍada's park. The place is usually referred to in the sūtras as “Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's park,” and according to the Saṃghabhedavastu the Buddha used Prince Jeta's name in first place because that was Prince Jeta's own unspoken wish while Anāthapiṇḍada was offering the park. Inspired by the occasion and the Buddha's use of his name, Prince Jeta then offered the rest of the property and had an entrance gate built. The Buddha specifically instructed those who recite the sūtras to use Prince Jeta's name in first place to commemorate the mutual effort of both benefactors.\n2) Anāthapiṇḍada built residences for the monks, to house them during the monsoon season, thus creating the first Buddhist monastery. It was one of the Buddha's main residences, where he spent around nineteen rainy season retreats, and it was therefore the setting for many of the Buddha's discourses and events. According to the travel accounts of Chinese monks, it was still in use as a Buddhist monastery in the early fifth centuryCE, but by the sixth century it had been reduced to ruins.\n\n(Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-46622.html)\n\nSynonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}38Synonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}39<place> jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ
36<place> Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park37<place> Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park (Skt: jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ): \n1) One of the first Buddhist monasteries, located in a park outside Śrāvastī, the capital of the ancient kingdom of Kośala in northern India. This park was originally owned by Prince Jeta, hence the name Jetavana, meaning Jeta's grove. Anāthapiṇḍada agreed, and managed to cover all of the park except the entrance, hence the name Anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, meaning Anāthapiṇḍada's park. Inspired by the occasion and the Buddha's use of his name, Prince Jeta then offered the rest of the property and had an entrance gate built. The Buddha specifically instructed those who recite the sūtras to use Prince Jeta's name in first place to commemorate the mutual effort of both benefactors.\n2) Anāthapiṇḍada built residences for the monks, to house them during the monsoon season, thus creating the first Buddhist monastery. According to the travel accounts of Chinese monks, it was still in use as a Buddhist monastery in the early fifth centuryCE, but by the sixth century it had been reduced to ruins.\n\n(Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-46622.html)\n\nSynonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}38Synonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}
36<place> Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Jeta's Wood, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Prince Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park37<place> Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Jeta's Wood, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park, Prince Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park (Skt: anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ): \n1) One of the first Buddhist monasteries, located in a park outside Śrāvastī, the capital of the ancient kingdom of Kośala in northern India. This park was originally owned by Prince Jeta, hence the name Jetavana, meaning Jeta's grove. The wealthy merchant Anāthapiṇḍada, wishing to offer it to the Buddha, sought to buy it from him, but the prince, not wishing to sell, said he would only do so if Anāthapiṇḍada covered the entire property with gold coins. Anāthapiṇḍada agreed, and managed to cover all of the park except the entrance, hence the name Anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, meaning Anāthapiṇḍada's park. The place is usually referred to in the sūtras as “Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's park,” and according to the Saṃghabhedavastu the Buddha used Prince Jeta's name in first place because that was Prince Jeta's own unspoken wish while Anāthapiṇḍada was offering the park. Inspired by the occasion and the Buddha's use of his name, Prince Jeta then offered the rest of the property and had an entrance gate built. The Buddha specifically instructed those who recite the sūtras to use Prince Jeta's name in first place to commemorate the mutual effort of both benefactors.\n2) Anāthapiṇḍada built residences for the monks, to house them during the monsoon season, thus creating the first Buddhist monastery. It was one of the Buddha's main residences, where he spent around nineteen rainy season retreats, and it was therefore the setting for many of the Buddha's discourses and events. According to the travel accounts of Chinese monks, it was still in use as a Buddhist monastery in the early fifth centuryCE, but by the sixth century it had been reduced to ruins.\n\n(Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-46622.html)\n\nSynonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}38Synonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal byed tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}39<place> anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ51({'dul ba}) {khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyis mnyan yod kyi grong khyer dang thag nye ring 'tsham pa'i gnas su ston pa dang de'i 'khor nyan thos dang bcas pa rnams bzhugs yul gyi ched du brtsigs pa'i gtsug lag khang dang skyed mos tshal ha cang mdzes sdug dang ldan la skad grags che ba zhig gi ming / mnyan yod du yod pa'i b+har ma'i d+harma sA la dang nye ba na rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal dze ta ba na mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba'i shul yod cing / 'di'i lo rgyus gong du khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin zhes pa'i thad du gzigs/}

491. {ra dza ku Ti/} (Source: 《{dung dkar tshig mdzod chen mo }》{sha }96.25{las btus/}).
36<place> Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park37<place> Jeta's Grove, Anāthapiṇḍada's Park (Skt: jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ): \n1) One of the first Buddhist monasteries, located in a park outside Śrāvastī, the capital of the ancient kingdom of Kośala in northern India. This park was originally owned by Prince Jeta, hence the name Jetavana, meaning Jeta's grove. The wealthy merchant Anāthapiṇḍada, wishing to offer it to the Buddha, sought to buy it from him, but the prince, not wishing to sell, said he would only do so if Anāthapiṇḍada covered the entire property with gold coins. Anāthapiṇḍada agreed, and managed to cover all of the park except the entrance, hence the name Anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ, meaning Anāthapiṇḍada's park. The place is usually referred to in the sūtras as “Jetavana, Anāthapiṇḍada's park,” and according to the Saṃghabhedavastu the Buddha used Prince Jeta's name in first place because that was Prince Jeta's own unspoken wish while Anāthapiṇḍada was offering the park. Inspired by the occasion and the Buddha's use of his name, Prince Jeta then offered the rest of the property and had an entrance gate built. The Buddha specifically instructed those who recite the sūtras to use Prince Jeta's name in first place to commemorate the mutual effort of both benefactors.\n2) Anāthapiṇḍada built residences for the monks, to house them during the monsoon season, thus creating the first Buddhist monastery. It was one of the Buddha's main residences, where he spent around nineteen rainy season retreats, and it was therefore the setting for many of the Buddha's discourses and events. According to the travel accounts of Chinese monks, it was still in use as a Buddhist monastery in the early fifth centuryCE, but by the sixth century it had been reduced to ruins.\n\n(Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-46622.html)\n\nSynonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}38Synonyms: {dze ta'i tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {dze ta'i tshal skyabs myed pa la zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal khyim bdag mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med pa la zas sbyin pa'i kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal bu rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}, {rgyal rgyal byed kyi tshal mgon med zas sbyin gyi kun dga' ra ba}39<place> jetavanam anāthapiṇḍadasyārāmaḥ

Each is the leader of a nonhuman class of beings living in his realm. They are Dhṛtarāṣṭra, ruling the gandharvas in the east; Virūḍhaka, ruling over the kumbhāṇḍas in the south; Virūpākṣa, ruling the nāgas in the west; and Vaiśravaṇa (also known as Kubera) ruling the yakṣas in the north. Also referred to as Guardians of the World or World Protectors (lokapāla, 'jig rten skyong ba). (Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-43611.html)\n\nSynonyms: {rgyal po bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi po}38Synonyms: {rgyal po bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi po}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi}39<person> caturmahārāja42caturmahārāja, caturhārājakāyika42caturmahārājānaḥ43nā. cāturmahārājikāḥ, ṣaḍdevanikāyeṣu ekaḥ — ṣaḍ devanikāyāḥ tadyathā cāturmahārājikāḥ, trayastriṃśāḥ, yāmāḥ, tuṣitāḥ, nirmāṇaratayaḥ, paranirmitavaśavarttinaśca abhi.bhā.3.1; catvāro mahārājāno lokapālāḥ — viruḍhakaḥ, virūpākṣaḥ, dhṛtarāṣṭraḥ, vaiśravaṇaśca, teṣu bhavāḥ cāturmahārājikāḥ abhi.sphu.3.1.45Sanskrit: cāturmahārājika <Word/Term/Phrase>\n\n\nTibetan renderings of cāturmahārājika: {rgyal chen bzhi/}, {rgyal chen bzhi pa/}\n\ncāturmahārājika, rarely and doubtfully °jaka, once cāturmāhā°, adj., almost always with deva or devaputra (the noun rarely omitted), = prec, and catur° (= Pali cātu(m)mah°), belonging to the group of gods of the four ‘World-Guardians’, a class of kāmāvacara gods, see deva, and mahāraja(n): °jaka (but v.l. °jika), without noun deva, Mv i.263.15; in the rest, °jika, LV 46.19; 150.2; 266.4 f.; 401.8 f.; Mv i.31.10; 240.4 (v.l. °ja-kāyika); ii.2.8; 163.10; Divy 68.12; 83.6; 127.20; 219.8; 367.9; 554.4; Av i.5.1; 10.16, etc; Mmk 19.12; common. This is the most usual form. Once, at least, cāturmāhārājika, Mv ii.314.5 (prose, no v.l.). Also mahārājika, q.v.\n\nSee the full entry at:https://www.itlr.net/hwid:61521347{rgyal chen rigs bzhi'i bsdus ming /}55{rgyal chen rigs bzhi'i bsdus tshig}

43nā. mahārājikadevāḥ, cāturmahārājakāyikāḥ devāḥ abhi.ko.3.64; caturthyāṃ (pariṣaṇḍāyāṃ) catvāro mahārājāḥ svayaṃ prativasanti, tatparicārāśca \n atastasyāṃ cāturmahārājakāyikāḥ devā ityuktam abhi.bhā.3.64.45Sanskrit: mahārājikadeva <Word/Term/Phrase>\n\n\nTibetan renderings of mahārājikadeva: {rgyal chen bzhi pa'i lha/}\n\nBasically, this term is equal in meaning to cā̆turmahārājakāyika. 36<place> Heaven of the Four Great Kings37<place> Heaven of the Four Great Kings (Skt: cāturmahārājakāyika): One of the heavens of Buddhist cosmology, lowest among the six heavens of the desire realm (kāmadhātu, 'dod khams). Dwelling place of the Four Great Kings (caturmahārāja, rgyal chen bzhi), traditionally located on a terrace of Sumeru, just below the Heaven of the Thirty-Three. Each cardinal direction is ruled by one of the Four Great Kings and inhabited by a different class of nonhuman beings as their subjects: in the east, Dhṛtarāṣṭra rules the gandharvas; in the south, Virūḍhaka rules the kumbhāṇḍas; in the west, Virūpākṣa rules the nāgas; and in the north, Vaiśravaṇa rules the yakṣas. (Original glossary entry: https://84000.co/glossary/entity-43980.html)\n\nSynonyms: {rgyal bo bzhi'i lha yul}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha rnams}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha'i ris}, {rgyal chen rigs bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi'i ris}38Synonyms: {rgyal bo bzhi'i lha yul}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha rnams}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha'i ris}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris kyi lha}, {rgyal chen bzhi'i ris}, {rgyal chen rigs bzhi}, {rgyal po chen po bzhi'i ris}39<place> cāturmahārājakāyika

2the devas of the abode of the Four Great Kings2the gods in the Abode of the Four Great Kings {ri rab kyi bang rim bzhi dang, ri bdun gyi kha khyer dang, go la'i rlung gi gzhi sogs la gnas} reside on the fourth terrace of Mount Sumeru, on the crests of the seven mountain ranges, on the 'wind base of the gola' and so forth36<term> gods from the Heaven of the Four Great Kings, gods of the Heaven of the Four Great Kings37<term> gods from the Heaven of the Four Great Kings, gods of the Heaven of the Four Great Kings (Skt: cāturmahārājakāyikadeva, devāś cāturmahārājikāḥ): One of the heavens of Buddhist cosmology, lowest among the six heavens of the desire realm (kāmadhātu, 'dod khams).
